                                                                                                                                Damage Control

PIPE REPAIR

     17-29. Piping system leaks usually accompanies any large hole in the hull. Soft patches can seal holes and
     cracks in low-pressure lines and water lines. Install a soft patch on a pipe as follows (see 17-18):

              Opening is plugged with soft wood plugs or wedges (the flow of water must not be retarded by
                  driving an excessive amount of wood into the pipe).

              Trim plugs and wedges flush with the outside of the pipe.
              Wrap rubber sheeting over the damaged area and back it with light sheet metal held in place with

                  bindings of wire or marline.

     17-30. Stop minor pipe leaks with a jubilee patch (an adjustable strap with a flange on each edge). These
     can be made up as needed. The patch is shaped by bending sheet metal around a cylinder and turning out the
     flanges and then clamped in place (see Figure 17-19). The flanges may have to be reinforced as pressure
     increases (Figure 17-20).

                  Figure 17-18. Installing a Soft Patch on a Pipe

                  Figure 17-19. Jubilee Pipe Patches

17 December 2018  TC 4-15.51                                       17-15


---

Chapter 17

                                Figure 17-20. Three Types of Reinforced Clamps

EMERGENCY DAMAGE CONTROL METALLIC PIPE REPAIR KIT

     17-31. Most water, fuel, and gas lines can be repaired and restored to the system within 30 minutes if the
     contents of the emergency damage control metallic pipe repair kit are applied properly. In addition to repair
     or patching of piping, certain materials, which may be used to patch small cracks and ruptures in flat metal
     surfaces, are included in the kit. Materials in the kit may be obtained separately through appropriate supply
     channels whenever a need arises to replace them. You do not need to obtain another complete new kit. A
     complete kit contains the following materials:

              Four cans epoxy, resin, 400 grams each.
              Four cans liquid hardener, 100 grams each.
              Four cans paste resin, 300 grams each.
              Four cans paste hardener, 75 grams each.
              One piece woven roving cloth, 24” x 10 “.
              One piece void cover, 8” x 36”.
              One piece polyvinyl chloride (PVC) film, 36” x 72”.
              One chalk line, 1/8 pound.
              Four pairs of gloves.
              Two eye shields.
              Four wooden spatulas.
              One sheet of emery cloth, 9” x 11”.
              One instruction manual.

DESCRIPTION OF MATERIALS

     17-32. The following describes the basic materials found in the kit. The discussion of factors related to
     plastics is given to help you gain a better understanding of the kit and its use.

              Void Cover. The void cover is a resin-treated glass cloth that can be cut and formed to cover the
                  damaged area. It is sufficiently rigid to give support to the patch.

              Woven Roving Cloth. The woven roving cloth is made of a short-staple, glass fiber woven into
                  a thick, fluffy cloth. During the application of a plastic patch this cloth is coated with the resin-
                  hardener mixture and either wrapped around or placed over the damaged area. The glass cloth
                  provides the main strength of the patch and also provides a means of applying the resin-hardener
                  mixture.

              Film (PVC). The plastic film, referred to as PVC, is a thin transparent polyvinyl chloride material.
                  It is used as a separating film for the flat patch to prevent the patch from sticking to the backup

17-16       TC 4-15.51  17 December 2018


---

                                                                                                                          Damage Control

             plate or other supports. In the pipe patch, it is used to cover the entire patch and retain the activated
             resin around the patch. Kraft wrapping paper may be used as a substitute if necessary.
        Resins and Hardeners. The liquid and paste resins are of the epoxy type. The liquid and paste
             hardeners are chemical compounds used to harden the resins. The resins and the hardeners are
             packaged in premeasured amounts. For proper mixture and better results, mix the complete
             contents of the hardener in the smaller can with the complete contents of the resin in the larger
             can.

                                             CAUTION

               DO NOT mix hardener with resin until all preparations have been
               completed. DO NOT intermix liquid resin and paste hardener or paste
               resin and liquid hardener.

17-33. When the resins and the hardeners are mixed together, a chemical reaction occurs which causes the
mixture to harden (liquid mixture, approximately 12 minutes; paste mixture, approximately 17 minutes).
This reaction is exothermic, which means that heat is given off. For approximately 12 to 17 minutes the
temperature increases gradually until it reaches 120 degrees F to 135 degrees F. At this point a sudden sharp
rise in temperature is known as kick over. It is at this temperature that the resin-hardener mixture begins to
solidify and change color from gray to light brown. The peak temperature (350 degrees F) can be observed
through the external change of the patch. The resin-hardener mixture begins to cool slowly due to the poor
thermal conductivity of the materials. After kick over, the mixture continues to harden and increase in
strength. This process is referred to as curing. Approximately 30 minutes after kick over (the sharp rise in
temperature), the patch is strong and hard and cool enough to use. Pressure should not be restored to the
system until the patch has cured. The patch is considered sufficiently cured when the bare hand can be placed
on it without discomfort from heat.

17-34. Several factors contribute to the control of kick over. The most important factor is the temperature.
Both the initial temperature of the activated resin mixture and the temperature of the atmosphere, affect the
kick over time. However, of these two temperatures, the initial temperature of the activated resin has the
greater effect. When the temperature of the resin and the hardener prior to mixing is increased, the kick over
time decreases. Conversely, when the temperature of the resin and hardener prior to mixing decreases, the
kick over time increases. Knowledge of controlling kick over is necessary since it corresponds to application
of working time. This means that when the initial temperature of the mixture is 73 degrees F, the patching
material must be placed over the rupture within 12 minutes. Once the resin and the hardener are mixed
together, the chemical reaction cannot be stopped. Therefore, the patch should be completely applied before
kick over occurs.

17-35. Figure 17-21 shows the relationship of the kick over time of the resin temperature. If you know the
resin temperature at the time of mixing, you will be able to determine the amount of time available to apply
the patch before kick over occurs. You can see that if the resin temperature is 80 degrees (point A), the kick
over will occur in less time than if the resin temperature were 60 degrees (point B). The difference in resin
temperatures represents an application working time of 9 minutes versus 18 minutes.

       Note: If the initial resin temperature exceeds 80 degrees F the temperature should be reduced by
       artificial means to 73 degrees F prior to mixing. This lowering of the temperature allows for
       additional application working time.

17 December 2018  TC 4-15.51  17-17


---

Chapter 17

                      Figure 17-21. Resin Temperature Versus Kick Over Time Graph

MATERIALS REQUIRED FOR PIPE PATCH

     17-36. Table 17-2 shows approximate quantities of materials required for pipe patches. The top figure in
     the boxes shows quantities in the amount of resin and hardener mixture in grams. The second group of
     figures, immediately below is the dimensions in inches of woven roving cloth.

ADVANTAGES OF THE PLASTIC PATCH

     17-37. From the damage control viewpoint, the main advantages of the plastic patch are versatility,
     simplicity, effectiveness, speed of application, and durability. The plastic patch can be successfully applied
     to a variety of damaged surfaces (such as smooth edges or jagged protruding edges). Since the plastic has
     excellent adhesive qualities it can be readily applied to steel, cast iron, copper, copper-nickel, brass, bronze,
     and galvanized metals. The plastic materials and the plastic patch may be readily prepared and applied. By
     following the instructions outlined in the instruction manual included in the kit, a person with little or no
     experience can readily prepare the materials and apply a plastic patch. Applying a plastic patch is comparable
     to applying a battle dressing used in first aid. If the materials are properly prepared and the application
     procedures are duly followed, the plastic patch will be 100 percent effective. If leakage occurs through a
     plastic patch, it is likely that proper preparation and application procedures have not been followed. The
     speed of application will vary somewhat with the size and type of rupture and also with local working
     conditions. When proper preparation procedures are followed, an inexperienced crew, who has had a
     minimum amount of training, can apply a simple patch to a 4-inch pipe in
     10 minutes or less. The type and size of the structure to which the patch is applied do not materially affect
     the time involved in patching. However, some types of damage may require more initial preparation.

APPLICATION OF PLASTIC PATCH

     17-38. When applying a plastic patch, you will see that as the individual patch materials are applied, the
     patch becomes progressively wider. Figure 17-22 shows the relative positions of the patch materials to one
     another.

                  Figure 17-22. Relative Positions of Patch Materials

17 December 2018                    TC 4-15.51                                   17-19


---

Chapter 17

     17-39. The buildup in the patch length during application must be considered initially when determining the
     length of the patch to be applied. Where suitable, allow the patch to extend at least 4 to 5 inches on either
     side of the rupture. In addition to the size of the rupture, the width of the patch may also depend on the
     location of the rupture in the pipe system. For example, an elbow rupture may require a wider patch than the
     same size rupture would require in a straight section of pipe. Certain specific preparations must be made
     prior to the actual application of the plastic patch. These are as follows:

              Secure or isolate the rupture area in the piping system.
              Remove all lagging.
              Clean the area around the rupture and remove all grease, oil, dirt, paint, and other foreign matter.

                  If grease or oil is present, use an approved solvent such as ethyl chloroform. If this solvent is not
                  available, scrape and wipe the surface until it is clean. When a clean surface is obtained, the
                  surface may be further abraded for better adhesion. An abrasive cloth is furnished with the kit.
              Make sure that the entire pipe surface is dry.
              Where practical, simplify the rupture by bending or removing irregular projections. This may be
                  accomplished by cutting or burning.

                                                 CAUTION

                    It is of the utmost importance that no explosive conditions exist prior to
                    using spark-producing tools or burning equipment.

     17-40. Determine the materials that will be required, such as the amount of woven roving cloth and the
     amount of resins and hardeners. For example, a 2-inch rupture in a 2-inch diameter pipe will require
     500 grams of activated resin and a piece of woven roving cloth that is at least 25 inches long. Cut the woven
     roving cloth wide enough to extend at least 3 to 4 inches on either side of the rupture.

SIMPLE PIPE PATCH

     17-41. The following are step-by-step procedures for applying the simple pipe patch.
              Put on eye shields and gloves. Then open the liquid resin can and the liquid hardener can.
              Add hardener to the resin and mix thoroughly for approximately 2 minutes or until a uniform gray
                  color is observed. (Note that the entire contents of the liquid hardener in the smaller can, is the
                  correct proportion for mixing with the entire contents of the larger can of liquid resin.)
              Coat both sides of the void cover with the resin-hardener mixture and tie the void cover over the
                  rupture with chalk line as shown in Figure 17-23, step A.
              Lay the woven roving cloth on a clean flat surface. Starting at one end of the cloth, pour on resin-
                  hardener mixture and spread evenly over the entire surface of the cloth using the spatula provided
                  in the kit. Only one side of the woven roving cloth needs to be impregnated. Be sure that the
                  edges are well impregnated with the resin-hardener mixture.
              Center the woven roving cloth over the void cover with the impregnated side toward the pipe.
                  Wrap it around the pipe not less than three turns and preferably not more than four turns
                  (see Figure 17-23, step B).
              Wrap the PVC film around the entire patch making at least two complete turns. Tie the PVC film
                  with the chalk line, starting from the center of the patch and working toward one end, making 1/2-
                  inch spacing between spirals (see Figure 17-23, step C). Tie this end securely but do not sever the
                  line.
              Make one spiral back to the center of the patch, then working to the opposite end, form the center
                  of the patch. Make 1 1/2-inch spacing between spirals and again secure the line. After 30 to 40
                  minutes the patch should be sufficiently cured to restore the pipe to service.

     17-42. Remember that for best results the temperature of the liquid resin and the liquid hardener should be
     approximately 70 degrees F before mixing. The patch will cure in approximately one hour from the initial
     mixing time. After an hour, pressure may be restored to the piping system. In emergencies, if the temperature

17-20  TC 4-15.51  17 December 2018


---

                                                                                                                          Damage Control

of the resins and the hardeners is below 50 degrees F, applying external heat with hot-air heaters may
accelerate kick over. However, the external heat must be applied gradually because excessive application of
heat will cause the plastic patch to be extremely porous.

                  Figure 17-23. Simple Pipe Patch

                               Fast Rescue Boat

         This is a guideline and describes general requirements, equipment, training protocol, and
         other information that is not specific to individual vessels or davit types.

  SECTION I –GENERAL INFORMATION

COMMAND BRIEF

     18-1. A Command brief and individual team safety brief shall be conducted before and after each launch and
     recovery evolution. These briefs shall discuss risk assessment, a clear sequence of events, and mission specific
     safety procedures. Briefs should also create a climate for learning and encourage feedback by constructive
     critique.

     18-2. The Command brief should include, the Vessel Master, the Chief Engineer Officer, Safety Observer,
     OIC/NCOIC on the Deck, Coxswain, Sea Painter Tender and any other essential personnel the Vessel Master
     deems necessary.

INDIVIDUAL TEAM BRIEF

     18-3. Individual team briefs, or local briefs, shall consist of personnel assigned to a team. At a minimum the boat
     launch and recovery team brief shall consist of the Safety Observer, Davit Operator and additional members of
     the Rescue Boat Operations Bill. The boat crew “team” brief shall, at a minimum, include the coxswain and
     rescue boat crew members.

AFTER ACTION REVIEW

     18-4. All AAR’s shall include lessons learned and should be based on improvement of the evolution. Topics
     discussed should include both positive and negative results of the completed evolution.

             Note: These briefs are critical, especially during time-sensitive evolutions such as “Man Overboard.”
             Simply put, time-critical evolutions do not provide relief from complying with the guidance established
             in this manual. Indeed, familiarity with this manual ensures that boat evolutions executed in a
             timeframe that supports operational tasking remain both safe and effective.

ARMY SAFETY MANAGEMENT SYSTEM

     18-5. Technical skills are imperative to boat launch and recovery operations. However, these skills alone will
     not ensure safety. Shipboard rescue boat evolutions are inherently dangerous, and therefore require coordinated
     teamwork to achieve operational success and maximize safety. It is crucial that vessel crews use the Army Safety
     Management System (ASMS) and team coordination principles found in section (a) while conducting shipboard
     launch and recovery operations.

RISK ASSESSMENT

     18-6. ASMS models shall be a part of evolution planning and shall be included during the required safety
     briefings. All members attending a briefing shall have input on risk assessment models. The ultimate decision to
     conduct the evolution rests with the Vessel Master with input from the persons included in the brief. Risks shall
     be re-assessed as situations change. AR 385-10 will be referenced when completing DD Form 2977.

17 December 2018  TC 4-15.51  18-1


---

Chapter 18

ENVIRONMENTAL CONDITION RISK MANAGEMENT ASSESSMENT

     18-7. The Vessel Master shall also consider the specific operating characteristics of each rescue boat in relation
     to the prevailing weather/sea conditions when making risk assessment decisions.

     18-8. Due to the many variances in shape, steepness and period for seas and swells, no policy can cover all the
     variables. The conditions during the launch and recovery portions of a rescue boat operation, as well as the size
     and reaction to sea/swell of any vessel that will be approached by the rescue boat, are limiting factors in the risk
     assessment. In most cases, once away from the vessel, rescue boats can safely operate in greater seas than those
     in which they can be launched or recovered.

     18-9. Vessels may encounter situations where a mission requirement exists in sea conditions exceeding the
     guidance given above. In these situations, the Vessel Master shall conduct a final launch/no launch risk assessment
     after the initial assessment. This assessment shall include, but not be limited to such factors as: mission urgency,
     wave shape and period, seas, wind, icing, visibility, and temperature.

     18-10. Experience and proficiency of the boat launch and recovery detail as well as fatigue, duration of mission,
     improving vs. deteriorating weather conditions, and other factors will play a large part in the launch/no launch
     decision. While darkness may be considered in this decision, it is expected that a vessel can safely launch, recover
     and operate boats at night.

ERROR TRAPPING

     18-11. Safety briefings must empower subordinates to monitor circumstances and report situations that differ
     from planned evolutions, hazardous conditions, or are unclear. By discussing risk, personnel are more aware of
     potential hazards and how best to mitigate them. It is extremely important for a system of notification in
     communicating perceived risk to be in place and known by every team member.

PROPER WATCH ATMOSPHERE

     18-12. Typically, well-articulated, standard voice commands, and hand signals are all that are required for well-
     trained personnel to conduct safe and effective shipboard launch and recovery operations. Standard hand signals
     are contained in Enclosure 2. All crewmembers must ensure the team observes proper practices and refrains from
     undue frivolity and/or loud boisterous behavior, while the Safety Observer and OIC/NCOIC provide the necessary
     oversight. There should be no excitement or confusion while conducting evolutions. If confusion does exist in
     any member of the team, that member must be empowered to ask questions and seek clarification.

PERSONAL PROTECTIVE EQUIPMENT

     18-13. Shipboard launch and recovery evolutions are inherently dangerous. Using davits and winches to move
     rescue boats on moving platforms can easily injure personnel and damage the vessel. The use of Personal
     Protective Equipment (PPE) by all personnel on deck is the first line of defense against injury.

PERSONAL FLOTATION DEVICES

     18-14. Only approved PFDs shall be worn by all personnel conducting launch and recovery operations. Inflatable
     personal flotation devices shall NOT be worn by launch and recovery personnel during boat launch and recovery
     evolutions.

HARDHATS/HELMETS

     18-15. A hardhat provides impact protection to a wearer’s head. Some models protect the back of the neck from
     falling debris as well. Hardhats shall be worn at all times when conducting overhead operations. Hardhats shall
     meet American National Standards Institute (ANSI) standard Z89.1- 2003 or newer.

     18-16. In addition, hardhats/helmets shall:

18-2  TC 4-15.51  17 December 2018


---

                                                                                                        Glossary

 Be outfitted with a chin strap (helmets) that is worn at all times.
 Be kept clean and free of unauthorized paint and stickers to allow for inspection for cracks and other

     deformities. The wearer’s name and rank are authorized to be on the back of the hardhat/Helmet.
 Hardhats can be color coded to quickly identify the wearer’s assignment on deck. example:
 White: Safety Supervisor (SO)
 Yellow: Deck Officer in charge/Noncommissioned Officer in charge (OIC/NCOIC)
 Brown: Davit Operator (DO)
 Blue: Qualified Deck Seaman (DS)
 Green: Un Licensed Seaman (BI)
 Red (helmet): Rescue Boat Coxswain

SAFETY FOOTWEAR

      18-17. Wearing safety footwear is mandatory when conducting boat launch and recovery operations.
      Footwear should be comfortable and well-fitting with non-skid soles. Boots are recommended for better ankle
      support. Like gloves, different styles of boots may be necessary to meet operational and climate requirements.

GLOVES

      18-18. While wearing gloves during boat evolutions is highly discouraged, when environmental conditions
      make it prudent, personnel may wear leather palm work gloves or gloves made of synthetic materials. Gloves
      shall fit snuggly to help reduce the possibility of becoming fouled and have no loose appendages.

SAFETY HARNESS OR BELT

      18-19. Certain circumstances may require launch and recovery personnel to mitigate the risk of falling
      overboard. In these cases, a full body safety harness or a safety belt shall be used in conjunction with appropriate
      PPE.

ADDITIONAL GEAR

18-20.    In addition to the PPE already mentioned, the following gear should be considered for issue:
            Coveralls (Regular and/or insulated)
           Hard hat/Helmet liner
           Foul weather work coat
           Knife
           Rain gear
           Thermal socks
           Goggles
       

MANNING GUIDELINES

      18-21. Due to the unique characteristics of each class of vessel it is difficult to designate and describe every
      boat launch and recovery manning position in detail in this manual. Individual vessels are responsible for
      promulgating specific bills as per the Vessels layout. Below are the described positions and responsibilities most
      commonly used during launch and recovery procedures.

SAFETY OBSERVER

      18-22. The Safety Observer (SO) is responsible for the overall safety of the boat launch and recovery operation
      and shall be a qualified rescue boat operator.

17 December 2018    TC 4-15.51                                                                          18-3


---

Chapter 18

OIC/NCOIC

     18-23. OIC/NCOIC is responsible for the overall launch and recovery operation of the rescue boat and shall be
     a qualified rescue boat operator.

DAVIT OPERATOR

     18-24. The Davit Operator (DO) is responsible for safe and efficient operations of the installed vessels davit(s).
     This position requires the operator to be intimately familiar with applicable Technical Publications and a
     certified/licensed operator.

SEA PAINTER TENDER

     18-25. Passes, recovers, and adjusts the sea painter as directed by the OIC/NCOIC. The sea painter tenders must
     be well trained.

LINE TENDERS

     18-26. Line Tenders (LT) are responsible for tending frapping lines (attached to the falls) and tending lines
     (attached to the boat) to prevent the rescue boat from swinging wide as the ship rolls and to keep the rescue boat
     parallel to the vessel. The line tenders must be well trained and able to prevent their lines from slipping off of
     deck fittings.

DECK HAND

     18-27. The Deck Hand (DH) is proficient in the duties of Line Tender (LT) and Sea Painter Tender (SPT) to
     serve in either position as directed. He/she may also serve in other various deck positions as directed (i.e. tending
     fenders).

QUALIFICATION AND CERTIFICATION REQUIREMENTS

INTRODUCTION

     18-28. The Vessel Master/Chief Engineer are responsible for ensuring all launch and recovery team personnel
     are certified and licensed in accordance with section (d) and AR 56-9. The required training is found below.
     Launch and recovery personnel shall satisfactorily complete three day and one night launch and recovery every
     six months, per davit type, to maintain certification/licensed currency.

CERTIFICATIONS

     18-29. All Vessel Masters, Mates, Safety Observers, OIC, NCOIC and Coxswains aboard a vessel equipped with
     a rescue boat must have a current/valid Rescue Boat Certification.

GENERAL SAFETY REQUIREMENTS

     18-30. The following requirements are generic in nature and applicable to all vessels during launch and recovery
     operations.

INSPECTIONS/REQUIREMENTS

     Prior to a rescue boat launch or recovery the Chief Engineer shall ensure that all tests, load tests, inspections and
     routine maintenance are complete and up to date in accordance with references (f), and the appropriate Technical
     Publications.
     18-31. All weight handling equipment shall be maintained in accordance with references (f). All slings, hoists,
     bails, davits and associated equipment shall be visually inspected immediately prior to, and after, every use.
     Improvised slings are not authorized for boat launch and recovery operations.

18-4  TC 4-15.51  17 December 2018


---

                              Glossary

18-32. Boat gripes shall be installed in accordance with ship’s plans or drawings.

18-33. Frapping lines and tending lines shall be constructed of fiber or a synthetic material that resists stretch or
of significant diameter to reduce stretch.

18-34. Davit/Crane operational function checks shall be completed prior to use in accordance with specific vessel
class davit/crane type technical publications or operating manuals.

18-35. The Vessel Master shall ensure Risk Assessment models shall be conducted for each evolution and
updated as conditions change. The results of the risk assessment shall be retained with the boat operations
checklist.

18-36. All members involved in Boat Launch and Recovery operations shall complete initial or recurring training
prior to full qualification in accordance with section (E). The unit Company Commander may defer this
requirement until such time that approved training can be scheduled.

18-37. Boat launch and recovery operations shall not commence without prior permission of the Vessel Master.

18-38. The SO and OIC/NCOIC shall be in their assigned positions prior to un-griping the boat.

18-39. Never un-gripe a rescue boat without the falls or a crane attached. Never release the falls without the
gripes attached. The command to un-gripe the rescue boat shall not be given until the evolution calls for the
release ensuring that all safety procedures have been followed.

18-40. Never exceed the Working Load Limit (WLL) of the davit in accordance with associated Technical
Publications.

18-41. Place the vessel on the course and speed that minimizes pitch and roll and provides the best available lee
from the wind and seas. This optimizes safety of personnel and minimizes the possibility of damage to the boat
during boat launch and recovery operations. For most vessels, safe speed is approximately (bare steerageway) 4–
8 knots.

18-42. At a minimum, units shall man the following four positions for all rescue boat launch and recovery
operations: Safety Observer, OIC/NCOIC, Sea Painter Tender and the Davit Operator. OIC/NCOIC and DO may
be combined when ship's configuration and construction allow for a full view of the evolution, davit/crane, and
boat from the davit controls. The SO shall have no other assigned duties for the duration of the launch or recovery
evolution.

18-43. Only authorized personnel involved in the evolution are allowed on the boat deck during launch and
recovery operations.

18-44. The 1MC is the primary means of notification that a rescue boat launch or recovery evolution is taking
place. Radios are acceptable provided that all necessary information can be passed.

18-45. Personnel shall not wear watches, jewelry, rings or baggy clothing, or other items which may be caught
in rigging or machinery.

18-46. Under no circumstance shall personnel place themselves under or turn their back to a suspended load.

18-47. When engaged in boat launch and recovery operations ensure all lines are properly staged and free from
potential fouling.

18-48. Emergency rescue boat launch and recovery operations (i.e., Man Overboard) should be taught and
reviewed during the licensing process and emergency drills for OIC/NCOIC, DO, Coxswain and SO personnel.

18-49. Chemical lights may be attached to critical components (sea painter, blocks, etc.) for night operations or
as appropriate.

18-50. Gloves worn while handling lines should be specifically designed for line handling. (See Personal
Protective Equipment)

18-51. Rescue boats when moored alongside, or cribbed at the rail (if authorized), shall be inspected hourly (or
as needed) to ensure tending lines are secure and the boat is not getting damaged.

17 December 2018  TC 4-15.51  18-5


---

Chapter 18

     18-52. The rescue boat shall never be launched with a bow down angle.

     18-53. A sea painter shall be used for launch and recovery evolutions when the vessel is making way. While the
     vessel is not making way, the sea painter is optional provided there is no current. The sea painter shall be attached
     to the rescue boat’s bow post if fitted, if not the trailer eye-bolt by means of a pendant with a quick disconnect
     connecting the bow post or trailer eye-bolt to the sea painter from the vessel. A lanyard should be attached to the
     pendant and tended from the bow of the rescue boat during launch and recovery operations. A wooden toggle is
     also authorized in lieu of a pendant as a quick release and shall be attached in such a way that when pulled the
     sea painter releases from the bow post.

     18-54. Interface Testing & Evaluation (IT&E) shall be conducted on the rescue boat launch and recovery system
     anytime a component within the system is altered from the original configuration. Components include: davits
     and associated equipment, the rescue boat, hoisting slings, or any other altered or trial piece of equipment.

TECHNICAL MANUALS

     18-55. All applicable Technical Manuals (TMs) shall be maintained onboard the vessel and referred to for
     Rescue Boat and davit type specifics. Emergency operating instructions, general operating instructions,
     maintenance procedures, safety precautions, and the safe working load.

SHIPBOARD LAUNCH AND RECOVERY GUIDANCE

INTRODUCTION

     18-56. It would be incredibly difficult to sufficiently describe, in writing, instructions and or guidance on how
     to launch and recover all rescue boats attached to the various US Army Vessels in the fleet in every weather
     condition. What follows is general guidance that shall be taken into consideration along with on scene conditions
     and individual vessel handling. This section is not intended to over-rule the Vessel Master, or other on-scene
     personnel, but to provide guidance to supplement their baseline knowledge and experience.

     18-57. As a general rule, most vessels use a ship’s speed between 4 and 6 knots when launching or recovering
     boats. Small vessels may have to take all way off to safely conduct launch and recovery based on hull design or
     limited personnel. Engine exhaust and cooling raw water discharge location in the vessels hull, may also require
     crews to secure the engine on the engaged side of the vessel used in launching or recovering the rescue boat. As
     an example, the LSV might need to secure the starboard main diesel engine during launch and recovery
     operations.

     18-58. With regards to headings, there are two practices used by most of the fleet: running down-swell to provide
     a more favorable ride for the personnel involved, or taking the seas on the opposite bow to provide a lee for the
     rescue boat during launch and recovery. For example, when launching the port boat on a LCU, the vessel typically
     takes the seas on the starboard bow or quarter. Also, vessels operating on rivers must take the current into account.
     Vessels normally run upriver to stem the current during boat launch and recovery operations

     18-59. The principles of the Army Safety Management System described in this Manual and in reference (a)
     shall be fully utilized to determine the level of risk and best course of action for launching or recovering the boat.

             Note: No written guidance or instruction can outweigh patience, common sense, and the practice of
             prudent seamanship. The safety of the crew, rescue boat, and vessel will always take priority when
             determining how and when to launch and recover rescue boats.

PITCH AND ROLL LIMITS

     The following pitch and roll limits shall only be exceeded during urgent Search and Rescue or other missions that
     in the judgment of the Vessel Master are significant enough to warrant the increased risk associated with the
     launch and recovery evolution. Pitch and roll limitations for each vessel are 5 degrees or calm seas (<2 feet).

18-6  TC 4-15.51  17 December 2018


---

                                                                                                                                                Glossary

GENERAL COURSE AND SPEED RULES

     18-60. General Course and Speed parameters are described below for each class of vessel. They are intended as
     basic guidance and to familiarize command and other personnel who may not have familiarity with the launch
     and recovery procedures for certain classes of vessels. All sea states in this section refer to the Beaufort scale and
     all references to “degrees” are relative. Recommended speeds are speed through water, not over ground. Vessel
     commands shall consider all environmental factors when determining speed through the water and its effect on
     the rescue boat during launch and recovery operations.

     18-61. The guidance in this section shall not supersede safe navigation of the vessel.

LSV AND LCU

     18-62. Due to the hull form and current davit components, LSVs and LCUs should take the seas off of the
     opposing bow (030 to 060 degrees or 300 to 330 degrees) to launch and recover rescue boats. The ideal speed is
     dead-in-the-water (bare steerage way) to 6 knots or both engines all ahead dead slow. For the starboard davit,
     taking seas off of the port bow or port quarter are the best options. Operations in heavier seas often require the
     seas to be taken off of the port bow.

LSV

     18-63. Embarkation / debarkation is located at the 0-2 level at the rail with the sponson level with the top of the
     deck, The rescue boat is hogged in and tied off on cleats utilizing 2 tag lines. Sea state, 3 feet max and speed 4-8
     knots or all ahead dead slow.

LCU

     18-64. Embarkation / debarkation is located at the main deck level with the sponson level with the top of the
     gunwale. The rescue boat is hogged in and tied off on cleats utilizing two tag lines. The sea painter goes through
     the #4 closed chock and is secured to a cleat. The maximum sea state is two feet and speed is 4-8 knots or all
     ahead dead slow.

LARGE TUG

     18-65. Always take into account the tow, charted depth of water, traffic, available space, and potential hazards
     before deploying the rescue boat.

     18-66. Seas off the opposing bow, 30 -60 degrees or 300-330 degrees are preferred to launch and recover rescue
     boats. The maximum sea state is 0-1 foot, vessel speed is dead in the water to 1 knot.

     18-67. For the port davit, taking seas off the starboard bow or starboard quarter are the best options. Operations
     in heavier seas often require the seas to be taken off of the starboard bow.

     18-68. Embarkation/debarkation is located at the 4 bitt. The rescue boat is hogged in and tied off above the
     fendering and out of the water utilizing two tag lines. The sea painter is attached to the 3 bitt.

SEA PAINTER GUIDANCE

     18-69. The Sea Painter is a key component for davit-based launch and recovery operations. Several mishaps
     have taken place due to improperly rigged or mishandled sea painters. This section standardizes the configuration
     of the sea painter on all vessels that employ the sea painter.

SEA PAINTER CONFIGURATION

     18-70. For rescue boats with a bow post the sea painter shall consist of two lines (the sea painter line and a
     pendant) and stainless steel quick release hardware with a rated capacity that exceeds the weight of the rescue
     boat or a wooden toggle (fid) rigged for a quick release.

17 December 2018  TC 4-15.51  18-7


---

Chapter 18

SEA PAINTER LINE

     18-71. The majority of line making up the sea painter will be the line that is attached to the parent vessel. This
     line will be attached to the vessel forward of the davit/boat deck and will be sized appropriately for the rescue
     boat, ideally 2-3” double braided nylon. Length of this line will vary based on variables in the construction and
     layout of the parent vessel. The line should also be whipped to alert the sea painter tender (SPT) as to the length
     required for various evolutions (i.e. launching/recovering the boat and cradling the boat).
     18-72.

SEA PAINTER PENDANT

     18-73. The sea painter pendant shall be a line that is the same size as the sea painter line. It shall be permanently
     spliced with a thimble in place to the trailer eye-bolt on the bow of the rescue boat or the bow post. The length of
     the pendant shall be long enough to be clipped or made off to the bow-post when the rescue boat is away from
     the vessel. Additionally, the pendant shall have a retrieval line spliced into the end of the pendant and then made
     off to the bow post so that crew-members can retrieve the pendant after the rescue boat is away from the vessel.

             Note: The pendant is only required for rescue boats that only have a trailer eye bolt.

CONNECTION HARDWARE

     18-74. Using a stainless steel quick connect is the preferred method of connecting the rescuer boat to the ship’s
     sea painter due to the ease of use and quick disconnect capability. The quick disconnect must be closed properly
     and can only open by pulling at a 90deg angle from the sea painter. However, using a fid is an acceptable option,
     and may be preferred by vessels operating in extreme cold weather where the boat crewmember’s dexterity could
     be impaired due to temperature.

  SECTION 2 –SINGLE POINT (SLEWING) DAVIT

INTRODUCTION

     18-75. This chapter describes general design features as well as launch and recovery procedures for single point
     davits. Detailed guidance relating to specific davits and their capabilities can be found in the appropriate technical
     publication.

18-8  TC 4-15.51  17 December 2018


---

                                  Glossary

GENERAL DAVIT DESIGN

                                  Figure 18-1 Diagram of a Typical Single Point Davit

LAUNCH PROCEDURES

LAUNCH

     18-76. This section provides an overview of rescue boat launch and recovery operations for vessels equipped
     with a single point davit. This section is not intended to cover all circumstances that vessels might encounter in
     the course of conducting operations. However, the safety rules covered in this Manual and other applicable
     publications, standard operating procedures, hand signals, and sound seamanship practices shall be followed
     whenever conducting rescue boat launch and recovery operations.

LAUNCH TASKS AND ACTIONS

     18-77. Bridge/Vessel Master:

17 December 2018      TC 4-15.51  18-9


---

Chapter 18

              Conduct required briefs with essential personnel and complete Rescue Boat Launch and Recovery
                  Checklist.

              Complete Risk Assessment Worksheet.
     18-78. SO, OIC/NCOIC

              a.Conduct individual team briefs.
              b.OIC/NCOIC establish communications with the bridge.

                                                   CAUTION!

                    Do not lay into the rescue boat without permission from the Vessel Master.

     18-79. Coxswain: completes communications check with the bridge
     18-80. 4. OIC/NCOIC ensures:

              Personnel are properly outfitted with safety gear.
              Personnel are qualified in their duties, or supervised by qualified member if training personnel.
              All lines are properly rigged.
              Spooling device checked.
              Sea painter rigged properly (discuss with bridge if not used).
              Boat plug in place, shore tie disconnected, dewatering ports secured, rescue boat cover removed, lower

                  unit in the up position.
              Fall hooks are properly seated to the rescue boat
              No gear adrift that hinders davit arm movement.
              Nothing is alongside the vessel or protruding from the vessel.
              Davit system energized.
              Preoperational test of winch/davit and rescue boat completed.
              “Manned and Ready” reported to the bridge.

                                                   WARNING:

                    The SO, OIC/NCOIC and DO shall not operate in any other capacity
                    than that assigned.

     18-81. Vessel Master:
              Ensures Engine room has secured overboard discharges as applicable.
              Ensures the vessel is placed on the best available course and speed that minimizes pitch and roll,
                  provides a lee from the wind and seas, and optimizes the safety of personnel while minimizing the
                  possibility of damage to the boat during the launch operation.
              Conducts communications check with rescue boat and OIC/NCOIC.
              Ensures the Boat Launch and Recovery Detail manned and ready, Risk assessment and checklist is
                  complete.
              Directs boat to be placed at the rail.
              Grants permission to launch the boat to the OIC/NCOIC when ready.

     18-82. OIC/NCOIC orders:
              The boat gripes released (after positive control of tending lines in place).
              The lifelines lowered and raised accordingly.
              Rescue boat winched up/hoisted until it will clear outboard shoe of the cradle (if applicable).
              Outboard rails dropped/released. Care must be taken not to place personnel under a suspended load.

18-10  TC 4-15.51  17 December 2018


---

                                                                             Glossary

 The Rescue boat slewed out until it is clear of the side.
 Rescue boat lowered to desired position (boat deck rail, main deck rail).
 Line Tenders to make up their tending lines to secure boat at the rail.

                                               CAUTION!

               Due to potential for damage, a Risk Assessment shall be conducted when
               contemplating leaving a rescue boat at the rail for an extended period of
               time. Factors to be considered include, but are not limited to: possible shock
               loading of davit(s)/hydraulics, forecasted weather conditions, vessel speed
               and anticipated maneuvers in relation to mission.

18-83. Line Tenders: Tend lines to prevent the swing of the rescue boat and keep the rescue boat parallel to the
vessel.

       NOTE: The number of Line Tenders is dependent on the vessel.

18-84. OIC/NCOIC:
        Gives order to “Lay into the Boat.” (See WARNING below)
        OIC/NCOIC to Coxswain “Ready in the Boat?”
        Coxswain lowers the lower unit, energizes batteries/electronics, and conducts radio check, if installed.
        Coxswain attaches engine kill switch to himself.
        Coxswain replies when ready, “Ready in the Boat.”

                                              WARNING:

               Care must be taken not to exceed the Safe Working Load (SWL) of the
               davit while loading boats at the rail. If necessary, only the Coxswain
               and/or engineer may enter the boat at this point.

18-85. OIC/NCOIC:
        Orders slewing out the rescue boat as necessary to ensure safe lowering of the rescue boat and boat
             crew.
        Orders the DO to winch down the rescue boat to the water.

       Note: Ensure the Coxswain is ready before lowering the rescue boat in the water.

        Orders the Sea Painter Tender to adjust the sea painter as necessary to keep the rescue boat positioned
             under the falls during the launch operation. It is imperative that the boat, once in the water, is towed
             by the sea painter and not by the davit; the sea painter shall not be adjusted at this point.

                                              WARNING:

               The most dangerous time for rescue boat launch operations is when
               the rescue boat becomes waterborne. For the safety of the boat crew,
               as well as the gear, every attempt should be made to release the whip
               as expeditiously as possible to prevent shock-loading the winch.

17 December 2018  TC 4-15.51                                                 18-11


---

Chapter 18

     18-86. Coxswain:
              Energizes navigational lights, as required.
              Starts the rescue boat.
              Orders the whip disconnected (or does it him/herself depending on personnel).

     18-87. 11. OIC/NCOIC: Orders additional personnel and/or gear into the boat if needed. This may be done at
     some other point in the evolution depending on vessel class and loading locations.

                                                   WARNING:

                    When releasing the whip maintain positive control of the hook.
                    Depending on class of vessel the whip may be under tension and in
                    some cases weighs upwards of 80 pounds.OIC/NCOIC shall direct
                    Line Tenders and the DO to hoist the whip clear of boat crew
                    personnel as quickly and safely as possible.

     18-88. Coxswain:
              Orders the release of the aft and forward tending lines. This can be done prior to lowering the rescue
                  boat in the water if necessary.
              Once the tending lines have been released and clear, shears the rescue boat away from the side of the
                  vessel by riding on the painter.
              Comes ahead to take the strain off the sea painter orders “Release the Sea Painter.” Engineer releases
                  the toggle or quick release.

     18-89. Sea Painter Tender: Then heaves around on the painter and makes the painter ready for rescue boat
     recovery.

     18-90. OIC/NCOIC:
              Orders the davit system secured.
              Orders life lines replaced and secured.
              Informs bridge that the boat deck is secure.
              Requests permission to secure the boat launch detail.
              Holds debrief/AAR.

RECOVERY PROCEDURES

     18-91. This section provides an overview of rescue boat recovery operations for vessels equipped with a single
     point davit. This section is not intended to cover all circumstances that vessels may encounter in the course of
     conducting operations. However, the safety rules covered in this Manual and other applicable publications,
     standard operating procedures, hand signals, and sound seamanship practices shall be followed whenever
     conducting rescue boat launch and recovery operations.

RECOVERY TASKS AND ACTIONS

     18-92. . Bridge/Vessel Master:
              Conducts required briefings with essential personnel and completes Rescue Boat Launch and Recovery
                  Checklist.
              Updates the Risk Assessment worksheet as necessary for the situation.

     18-93. Individual Stations:
              Conduct team briefings.
              Establish communications with other stations.

18-12  TC 4-15.51  17 December 2018


---

                                                                                                                                          Glossary

18-94. OIC/NCOIC ensures:
        Personnel are properly outfitted with PPE and safety gear.
        Personnel are qualified in their duties.
        All lines are properly rigged.
        Sea painter rigged properly with a heaving line secured to it as necessary.
        Boat deck is clear of unnecessary equipment.
        Nothing is alongside the vessel or protruding from the vessel.
        Davit and winch are energized.
        Outboard shoes/or cradle is dropped (if applicable).
        Conducts preoperational test of winch/davit.
        Slews out davit to appropriate position for vessel class and environmental conditions.
        Reports “Manned and Ready” to the bridge.

                                               WARNING

               The SO, OIC/NCOIC, and DO shall not operate in any other capacity
               than that assigned.

18-95. Vessel Master:
        Ensures the vessel is placed on the best available course and speed that minimizes pitch and roll,
             provides a lee from the wind and seas, and optimizes the safety of personnel while minimizing the
             possibility of damage to the rescue boat during the recovery operation.
        Informs the OIC/NCOIC and directs the boat to come alongside.

18-96. Coxswain:

18-97. Ensures the rescue boat is dewatered.
        Upon receiving order to come alongside, makes the approach to the vessel and takes station to receive
             the sea painter.
        Orders the sea painter passed.
        Once the sea painter has been connected, eases back on the throttle and allows the rescue boat to lay
             alongside the vessel. The sea painter tender ensures the rescue boat is towed alongside the vessel in
             the appropriate location preventing the rescue boat from being towed by the davit.
        Directs the passing or receiving of the tending lines.
        Disembarks passengers, crew and gear not essential to hoisting. This may be done at some other point
             in the evolution depending on vessel class.
        Passes the word “Ready in the Boat.”

18-98. OIC/NCOIC: Orders the Davit Operator (DO) to pay out slack in the whip.

                                              WARNING:

               When hooking the whip, maintain positive control of the block.
               Dependent on class of vessel, these blocks may be under tension
               and in some cases weigh upwards of 80 pounds. The Line Tenders
               and the DO shall keep the blocks well clear of the rescue boat crew
               until ordered to lower them.

18-99. Coxswain:

17 December 2018  TC 4-15.51  18-13


---

Chapter 18

              Once positive control of the block has been gained by the boat crew, orders “Hook it” (or does it
                  him/herself depending on personnel).

              Waits for DO to take excess slack out of falls.
              Secures the engine and raises the out-drive or outboard.
              Informs OIC/NCOIC ready in the boat.

                                                   WARNING:

                    Care must be taken not to exceed the SWL of the davit. If necessary,
                    only the Coxswain and/or engineer may remain in the boat at this
                    point.

                                                   WARNING:

                    The most dangerous time for rescue boat recovery operations is
                    when the rescue boat is connected to the whip while still waterborne.
                    For safety purposes every attempt should be made to hoist the
                    rescue boat expeditiously as possible to prevent shock-loading the
                    winch or tripping the Rescue Boat.

     18-100. . OIC/NCOIC orders:
              DO to adjust davit slew out to safely hoist the rescue boat.
              The rescue boat raised to the rail.
              DO to slew in as necessary to crib rescue boat to the vessels hull.
              Line Tenders to make off their tending lines.
              The boat crew to “Lay out of the Boat.”

     18-101. 9. Line Tenders: Tend their lines to prevent excess swing of the rescue boat and attempt to keep the rescue
     boat parallel to the vessel.

             Note: The number of Line Tenders is dependent on the class of vessel assigned.

     18-102. OIC/NCOIC orders:
              DO to slew out, as necessary, to safely clear the rescue boat from the hull.
              DO to winch rescue boat up to appropriate height to allow for slewing in to clear the cradle.
              DO to slew in as appropriate to cradle the rescue boat.
              The cradle or davit outboard shoes set as per ship’s plans (as appropriate).
              DO to lower the rescue boat to rest properly in the cradle, keel stops or davit shoes.
              Gripes set as per ship’s plans.
              DO to secure the davit for sea.
              DO to secure the power to the davit.
              Shore ties and rescue boat cover rigged as required.
              Conduct post operation PMCS; Rescue Boat washed, refueled and engine flushed, as required.
              The bridge notified that the rescue boat is “secured for sea.”
              Rescue Boat Detail secured.

     18-103. Hold after action reviews (AAR’s), team de-briefs and a bridge de-brief.
